**Rationale:** The course provides students with essential hands-on skills for using modern computing systems and digital tools, covering basic computer operations, system interfaces, and file management to build confidence and competence. It emphasizes practical use of productivity and communication tools such as MS Office, cloud platforms, and collaborative technologies through lab-based, real-world tasks, while also introducing basic programming concepts to develop computational thinking and problem-solving skills, thereby preparing students with practical computing competencies aligned with current academic and industry requirements.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Demonstrate understanding of basic computer operations, system interfaces, and productivity tools. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO2 | Apply MS Office, communication tools, and cloud platforms to create, edit, and manage digital content effectively. | Cognitive / Apply | PO5 | WK6 |  | EA1 |
| CO3 | Develop basic programs using introductory programming constructs to solve structured problems. | Cognitive / Apply | PO2 | WK3 | WP1 | EA1 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 1101 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Discrete Mathematics |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  None |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces the fundamental concepts of Discrete Mathematics that form the theoretical basis for algorithm design, data structures, and computational problem solving in computer science. The course covers core topics such as propositional and predicate logic, principles of mathematical reasoning and proof techniques, set theory, functions and relations, combinatorial analysis, basic probability theory, and graph theory. Emphasis is placed on developing logical thinking, formal reasoning, and abstraction skills, enabling students to model computational problems rigorously and apply discrete mathematical methods to the analysis and solution of problems encountered in computing and related disciplines.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Apply principles of mathematical and propositional logic to analyze and solve structured computational and mathematical problems. | Cognitive / Apply | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO2 | Apply systematic problem-solving techniques using counting methods and elementary probability models to analyze uncertain events. | Cognitive / Apply | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO3 | Model and solve practical and computational problems using graph-theoretic concepts | Cognitive / Apply | PO2 | WK3 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Introduction:** Logic and Proofs, Propositional Equivalences, Predicates and Quantifiers, Nested Quantifiers, Sets and Set Operations, Mathematical Inductions, Functions, Relation and  its properties. | 4 | 1 |
| **Number Theory, Counting and Cryptography:** Modular Arithmetic, representations of Integers, The Euclidean Algorithm, The basics of counting and pigeonhole principles, permutations and combinations, introduction to cryptography | 4 | 2 |
| **Discrete probability:** Introduction to probability theory, random variables, Bayes theorem, probability distributions and their types. | 2 | 2 |
| **Graph:** Introduction to graph, graph-related terminologies, representing graph and graph isomorphism, paths, reachability, connectivity, directed graphs, and shortest-path problems. | 2 | 3 |
| **Trees:** Introduction of trees, application of trees, tree traversal, Binary trees, binary search trees, spanning trees, minimal spanning trees, and undirected trees. | 2 | 3 |

|  Course Code:  EEE 1103 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Electrical Circuits Fundamentals |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  None |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces the fundamental principles of electrical circuit analysis, covering both direct current (DC) and alternating current (AC) systems. It develops a clear understanding of basic electrical quantities such as voltage, current, resistance, power, and standard measurement units. The course emphasizes essential circuit laws and systematic network analysis techniques, including KCL, KVL, Superposition, Thevenin’s, Norton’s, and Maximum Power Transfer theorems. It also provides foundational knowledge of magnetic circuits, RMS and instantaneous values, average power in R, L, and C combinations, and phasor representation of sinusoidal signals. Overall, the course builds a strong analytical foundation for solving practical electrical circuit problems in engineering applications.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Identify the functions of different components of electrical circuits, including R, L, and C elements. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |   |   |
| CO2 | Apply fundamental network theorems to analyze electrical circuits. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK3 |   |   |
| CO3 | Analyze the behavior and characteristics of various R, L, and C circuit combinations under DC and AC conditions. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO1 | WK3 | WP1 |   |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| Fundamental electrical concepts and measuring units; basic quantities (charge, voltage, current, resistance, power); unit systems and measurements. | 2 | CO1 |
| Direct Current (DC): Voltage, current, resistance, power; Ohm’s Law; series and parallel circuits; energy and power calculation. | 2 | CO1 |
| Circuit laws and analysis methods: KCL, KVL; mesh and nodal analysis techniques. | 2 | CO2 |
| Network theorems: Superposition theorem, Thevenin’s theorem, Norton’s theorem, Maximum Power Transfer theorem; introduction to magnetic circuits. | 2 | CO2 |
| Alternating Current (AC): Instantaneous and RMS values of current and voltage; sinusoidal waveforms; phasor representation; impedance concept. | 3 | CO3 |
| AC power analysis: Average power, power factor; behavior of R, L, and C circuits; series and parallel RLC combinations. | 3 | CO3 |

|  Course Code:  EEE 1104 |  |  Credit Hour:  2 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Electrical Circuits Fundamentals Sessional |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**   Sessional |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  None |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides hands-on experience in basic circuit design and experimental verification of DC network theorems, including KCL, KVL, Superposition, Thevenin’s, Norton’s, and Maximum Power Transfer theorems. Students also analyze RLC circuits to determine resonance frequency and observe practical circuit behavior. The course builds strong practical skills and prepares students for basic electronic circuit design.

| CO2 | Perform experimental verification and analysis of fundamental network theorems. | Psychomotor/ Precision | PO2 | WK2 |   |   |
| CO3 | Investigate and evaluate the characteristics of RLC circuits, including resonance behavior. | Affective/ Responding | PO4 | WK8 | WP1 | EA1 |

|  Course Code:  PHY 1105 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Physics Fundamentals |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** GED |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  None |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** Physics Fundamentals is designed to equip students with the quantitative reasoning and problem-solving skills essential for any technical career. The course covers core topics including electrostatics, steady electric current, magnetic fields and their interactions, electromagnetic induction, wave behavior, and the optical phenomena of interference, diffraction, and polarization of light. Through these subjects, students develop a rigorous analytical mindset and strong mathematical foundation that can be applied across a wide range of engineering and technology disciplines.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Understand the fundamental concepts of electrostatics, magnetism, and electromagnetic induction. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO2 | Analyze and explain wave-related phenomena, including interference, diffraction, and polarization of light. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply the governing laws of electric and magnetic fields to solve related problems. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |

| Faraday's Induction Law, Lenz's Law, Induced current and voltage, Self and mutual inductance, Inductor, Ideal transformer, Energy stored in a magnetic field | 2 | CO1, CO3 |
| Types of waves, Wave equation, Velocity and energy, Complex and standing waves, Resonance, Beats, Sound waves, Doppler Effect | 2 | CO2 |
| Huygens' Principle, Interference, Young's experiment, Michelson interferometer, Newton's rings, Holography, Fraunhofer and Fresnel diffraction, Diffraction grating, Polarization of light | 4 | CO2, CO3 |

**Rationale:** Physics Fundamentals Sessional provides students with hands-on experience in core experimental practices, including the use of graph papers (linear-linear, linear-log, and log-log), error calculation techniques, and the operation of essential electronic instruments such as the multimeter, oscilloscope, and signal generator. The course bridges theoretical knowledge with practical skills, preparing students to conduct and interpret scientific experiments with accuracy and confidence.

|  Course Code:  MAT 1107 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Differential and Integral Calculus |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** GED |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  None |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course is designed to advance students’ understanding of differential and integral calculus, to develop a solid foundation in functions, limits, continuity, ordinary differentiation, series expansions of functions, and the determination of maxima and minima of single-variable functions. The course further introduces partial differentiation, improper integrals, definite integrals, and multiple integrals. Upon successful completion, students will be able to identify and apply appropriate analytical techniques involving differentiation and integration to solve problems relevant to computer science, including applications in data mining, scientific computing, image processing, and the development of computer graphics, video games, and three-dimensional visualizations for simulation purposes.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Explain key mathematical concepts related to calculus and functions. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO2 | Apply fundamental theorems and techniques to solve calculus problems. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO3 | Analyze mathematical expressions and interpret their properties in various contexts | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK2 |  |  |

| Explicit functions, Implicit differentiation, Parametric equations; Derivatives and differentials, Significance of Derivatives & Differentials, Successive Differentiation of various types of Functions | 3 | CO1, CO2 |
| Rolle’s Theorem and Mean Value Theorem; Applications, Taylor’s and Maclaurin’s Theorems (finite and infinite forms); Leibnitz’s Theorem; Advanced differentiation problems | 3 | CO2, CO3 |
| Divergence and Convergence of Series. Partial differentiation, Tangent, normal, and curvature. Maxima and Minima of functions and applications; | 2 | CO2, CO3 |
| Integration overview, Applications of definite integrals; Improper integrals, Wallis formula; Beta & Gamma functions; Area under curves; Solids of revolution | 4 | CO2, CO3 |

|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  None |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** The course provides students with a strong foundation in programming principles and problem-solving techniques, emphasizing sequence, selection, iteration, memory management, data handling, and modularization. It equips students to develop efficient, readable, and maintainable code while applying computational logic to solve real-world mathematical and analytical problems. The course also prepares students for advanced studies in software development, algorithms, and related areas in computer science and engineering.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Demonstrate a solid understanding of basic programming concepts, control structures, and modular programming. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO2 | Apply structured programming techniques, including functions and basic data structures, for designing efficient and reusable solutions. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO3 | WK5 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply input/output operations and methods for interacting with external data sources effectively. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO5 | WK6 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Fundamentals of Programming:** Introduce the history of programming, generations of computers, machine/object code, translators, basic tokens, data types, and operators. | 2 | CO1 |
| **Branching:** Study conditional statements (if, switch) and nested branching to control program flow. | 2 | CO1 |
| **Iteration:** Understand loops (for, while, do-while) and their role in repeated execution of code blocks. | 3 | CO1 |
| **Pointers, Arrays, and Strings:** Learn pointer concepts, referencing/dereferencing, and handling of arrays and strings, and dynamic memory allocation. | 2 | CO2 |
| **Functions and Recursion:** Explore library and user-defined functions, parameters, return values, and recursive problem-solving approaches. | 2 | CO2 |
| **Structures and File Handling:** Understand structures for grouping data, and file operations for reading/writing text and binary data | 3 | CO3 |

**Rationale:** This course introduces students to the fundamental concepts of semiconductor physics and their practical applications. The course covers key electronic components including PN junction diodes, transistors, field effect transistors, operational amplifiers, and switching circuits. By developing a solid understanding of electronic circuit operations, students build the foundation necessary to advance into digital logic circuits and broader electronics-based disciplines.

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |

| CO2 | Explain the operation of transistors and their circuit configurations. | Cognitive/ Psychomotor | PO3 | WK5 |   |   |
| CO3 | Apply field effect transistors in relevant circuit applications. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO5 | WK6 |   |   |
| CO4 | Analyze op-amp based circuits and their applications. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK1 |   |   |

|  Course Code:  CSE 1205 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Digital System Design |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 1101 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces students to the foundational principles of digital logic and computing systems. The course covers essential topics including number systems, error detection codes, binary arithmetic, and 2's complement methods. Students explore Boolean algebra, logic gates, Boolean functions, and canonical forms, progressing to multilevel NAND/NOR circuits, exclusive-OR functions, and minimization techniques using algebraic simplification and Karnaugh maps. The course culminates in the design and analysis of combinational and sequential circuits, equipping students with the core skills needed for hardware design and digital system development.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Apply fundamental concepts of number systems, binary arithmetic, and logic gates in digital electronics. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO2 | Simplify binary logic circuits using Boolean algebra and Karnaugh map techniques. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO2 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO3 | Design and analyze combinational and sequential logic circuits. | Cognitive/ Create | PO3 | WK5 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| ----- | :---: | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| Number systems, error detection codes, binary arithmetic, 2's complement, Boolean algebra, logic gates, Boolean functions, switching circuits, input-output signals for gates | 2 | CO1 |
| Postulates of Boolean algebra, basic theorems and properties, Boolean functions (complement, duality), canonical and standard forms | 2 | CO1, CO2 |
| Boolean expression simplification using algebraic rules, Karnaugh map and Reed-Muller methods | 2 | CO2 |
| Combinational circuit design and analysis, multilevel NAND/NOR circuits, exclusive-OR functions, half and full adder/subtractor, binary parallel adder, encoder, decoder, multiplexer | 3 | CO1, CO3 |
| Sequential circuit fundamentals, latches, clock signals, clocked SR, JK and D flip-flops, timing diagrams, edge and level triggering | 3 | CO3 |
| Design and analysis of shift registers, memory devices and their applications | 2 | CO3 |

|  **Course Status:** GED |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  MAT 1107 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This is an introductory course in coordinate geometry and vector analysis aimed at equipping students with the tools to solve engineering problems. It bridges algebra and geometry through the study of lines and curves, providing essential methods for locating points in a plane. The concepts learned also have broad applications in trigonometry, solid geometry, computer graphics, and image processing.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Demonstrate a thorough understanding of coordinate systems and the geometry of straight lines. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO2 | Analyze and solve problems involving circles and vector quantities. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply key theorems and principles of vector calculus to engineering problems. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Coordinate Geometry:** Introduction to coordinate systems, including Cartesian and polar coordinates; the straight line and pair of straight lines; transformation of axes; circles and systems of circles; and the conic sections: parabola, ellipse, and hyperbola. | 4 | CO1 |
| **Three-Dimensional Coordinate Geometry:** Three-dimensional coordinate systems, coordinate transformations, distance between points, section formula, projections, direction cosines, and the equations of lines and planes. | 6 | CO1, CO2 |
| **Vector Analysis:** Fundamentals of vector algebra, including scalar and vector products; scalar and vector fields; applications of vectors in geometry; calculus of vector-valued functions; differentiation of vectors; gradient, divergence, and curl with physical interpretations; directional derivatives; vector integration; and theorems of Green, Stokes, and Gauss (Divergence) with illustrative examples. | 4 | CO2, CO3 |

|  Course Code:  HUM 1209 |  |  Credit Hour:  2 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |

|  **Course Title:** History of the Emergence of Bangladesh |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** GED |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  None |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides students with a comprehensive understanding of the historical roots and political, social, and cultural transformations that led to Bangladesh's independence. The course traces the journey from the struggle for provincial autonomy to the Liberation War of 1971, highlighting the heroic contributions of political leaders, student movements, and freedom fighters. Through this course, students develop a deeper appreciation of the significance of Bangladesh's emergence as a nation and cultivate a sense of national identity and patriotism.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Explain the historical roots and key events leading to the emergence of Bangladesh as an independent nation. | Cognitive/ Remember | PO4 | K8 |  |  |
| CO2 | Analyze the causes, events, and contributions of key figures in the Liberation War of 1971\. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO8 | K7 |  |  |
| CO3 | Evaluate the expectations and achievements of Bangladesh's independence in political, social, and cultural contexts. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO12 |   |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| Partition of India 1947: Lahore Conference, United Bangla Plan, Two-Nation Theory, creation of Pakistan and India, displacement crisis | 2 | CO1 |
| Language Movement of 1952: Bangla vs Urdu debate, agitation of 1948, 21 February 1952, constitutional status of Bangla; Economic, administrative, and political disparity between East and West Pakistan | 2 | CO1 |
| Military rule of Ayub Khan, Six-Point Movement and its results; Mass Upsurge of 1969, Yahya Khan, General Election of 1970, All Party Student Action Committee, Democratic Action Committee, withdrawal of Agartala conspiracy case | 2 | CO1 |
| Election results and aftermath; Historic Speech of 7 March, Non-cooperation Movement, Genocide of 25 March, Declaration and Proclamation of Independence; Mujibnagar Government | 2 | CO2 |
| Contributions of foreign nations to independence, 14 December tragedy, 16 December Victory; Government of Bangabandhu Sheikh Mujibur Rahman; 15 August and 4 November tragedies | 3 | CO2, CO3 |
| Liberation War expectations and achievements: a comparative study; July Movement and Revolution of 2024 and its significance in the context of Bangladesh's democratic aspirations | 3 | CO2, CO3 |

**Rationale:** This course introduces students to the foundational principles of chemical science, covering core topics including chemical bonding, solutions, phase rules and phase diagrams of mono-component systems, electrochemistry, chemical kinetics, and chemical equilibrium. The course equips students with essential analytical and theoretical skills applicable across engineering and applied science disciplines.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Explain the structure, bonding, properties, and reactions of organic and inorganic molecules. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO2 | Describe the characteristics and behavior of different liquid systems and phase relationships. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO3 | Analyze the kinetics, mechanisms, and equilibrium of chemical interactions. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK1 |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Chemical Bond:** Different types of chemical bonds, Properties of Ionic and Covalent Compound, and Modern Approaches of Covalent Bond. | 2 | CO1 |
| **Solution:** Types of Solution, Factors Influencing the Solubility of Substance. Mechanism of Dissolution; Solution of Gases in Liquids, Different Units of Concentration, Distribution Law and its Application; Properties of Dilute Solution, Raoult's Law \- its Application, Elevation of Boiling Point, Depression of Freezing Point and Osmotic Pressure. | 4 | CO2 |
| **Materials Chemistry:** Semiconductors: Doping, band gap theory, and applications in microchips. Polymers: Conducting polymers, optical fibers and biodegradable materials. | 2 | CO2 |
| **Electro-chemistry:** Electrolytes, Mechanism of Electrolytic Conduction, Transport Number and Electrolytic Conductance, Cell EMF. | 2 | CO2, CO3 |
| **Chemical kinetics and chemical equilibrium:** Rate of a Reaction, Factors Determining the Rate, Law of Mass Action, Evaluation and Characteristics of Equilibrium Constant of Reaction; The Lechatclier's Principle. | 4 | CO3 |

**Rationale:** This course is designed to provide students with a strong foundation in modern programming paradigms that emphasize modularity, reusability, and scalability. It covers essential OOP concepts, including classes, objects, inheritance, polymorphism, encapsulation, and abstraction, enabling students to model and implement complex systems effectively. By integrating theoretical understanding with practical programming applications, the course equips students to design and develop software solutions that adhere to best practices in software engineering, preparing them for advanced studies in software development, data structures, and system design.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |

| CO2 | Demonstrate understanding of inheritance, polymorphism, and abstraction in designing object-oriented systems. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO2 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply object-oriented principles to model and solve computational problems effectively. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK4 | WP1 | EA1 |

|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 1201 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces students to fundamental data structures and their applications in efficient algorithm design and problem-solving. Students will learn to organize, store, and manipulate data effectively using arrays, linked lists, stacks, queues, trees, graphs, and hash tables. Emphasis is placed on understanding the principles behind each data structure, analyzing time and space complexity, and implementing solutions that optimize computational efficiency. The course prepares students for advanced programming, algorithm design, and real-world software development challenges.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Understand and explain linear data structures such as arrays, strings, linked lists, stacks, and queues. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO2 | Understand and explain non-linear data structures such as trees, graphs, and hash tables.. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply appropriate data structures to design and implement efficient algorithms, analyzing time and space complexity. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO3 | WK5 |  |  |

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Data representation:** Internal data representation, Abstract data types, Ideas on linear and non-linear data structures, Elementary data structures. | 1 | CO1 |
| **Arrays:** Maximization, ordered lists, sparse matrices, representation of arrays. **Stacks, Queues and Recursion:** Different types of stacks and queues: Circular, dequeues, etc; evaluation of expressions, multiple stacks and queues. **Recursion:** Direct and indirect recursion, depth of recursion, Simulation of Recursion, Removal of recursion; Towers of Hanoi. **Linked Lists:** singly linked lists, linked stacks and queues, the storage pool, polynomial addition, equivalence relations, sparse matrices, doubly linked lists and dynamic storage management, generalized lists, garbage collection and compaction. | 6 | CO1 |
| **Trees:** Basic terminology, binary trees, binary tree representations, binary tree traversal; Extended binary trees: 2-trees, internal and external path lengths, Huffman codes/algorithms; threaded binary trees, binary tree representation of trees; Application of Trees: Heap tree, Max Heap, Min Heap, Heap sort, decision trees, games trees: Counting binary trees. **Graphs:** Introduction & its storing techniques in memory, traversing algorithm: BFS & DFS, spanning tree & algorithm for Minimal Spanning tree using Kruskal & Prims methods. **Symbol Tables:** static tree tables, dynamic tree tables; Hash Tables: Hashing functions overflow handling, theoretical evaluation of overflow techniques. **Files:** file, queries and sequential organizations: Indexing Techniques: Cylinder-surface indexing hashed indexes, tree indexing-B-trees; Tree indexing. | 7 | CO2, CO3 |

| COs | Instructional Strategy | Assessment Strategy |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Lecture, Practice, and Discussion | Class Test, Midterm Exam |
| CO2 | Lecture, Practice, and Discussion | Class Test, Final Exam |
| CO3 | Lecture, Practice, and Discussion | Assignment, Final Exam |

|  Course Code:  CSE 2105 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Computer Architecture |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 1205 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides students with a solid understanding of fundamental architectural techniques used to build today's high-performance processors and systems. Course topics include pipelining, superscalar, out of order execution, multithreading, caches, virtual memory, and multiprocessors. Some emphasis will be placed on hardware/software interaction to achieve performance. Issues affecting the nexus of architecture, compilers and operating systems will be briefly touched upon. Significant challenges face today's computer hardware industry. At the forefront of these challenges is the multi-core revolution. The transition from single processor designs to multi- core design requires hardware and software designers knowledgeable about a range of issues in parallel computing including hardware.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Illustrate hardware-software interaction to run the computer system. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO2 | Analyze various data communication between different components and processing unit. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO3 | Discuss detail functionality and connection architecture of different types of memories leading to high performance computing. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO4 | Illustrate hardware-software interaction to run the computer system. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO4 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Basic Concept:** Introduction to Computer Architecture, Computer Design and Computer Organization. Interaction of hardware with the system and application software, basic hardware components of computer system and bus structure, concept and analysis of performance. | 2 | CO1 |
| **Machine Instruction:** Concept of machine instruction, different parts of machine instruction, memory model maps for instruction, micro-operation, registers, addressing modes, I/O registers and instruction, program control status bits. | 2 | CO1 |
| **Processing Unit:** Components of processing units and their functions, general register organization for micro-operation, control word, pipelining, instruction pipelining and performance of pipelining. | 2 | CO2 |
| **I/O Organization:** Peripheral devices, I/O Interface, I/O bus module, synchronous data transfer and its different types, asynchronous data transfer, different modes of transfer, interrupt processing, direct memory access (DMA), I/O processor (IOP). | 3 | CO2 |
| **Memory Organization:** Concept of memory, memory hierarchy, main memory, functions of RAM and ROM, memory address map, memory connection with CPU, Cache memory and its performance, secondary storage, virtual memory. | 3 | CO3 |
| **Composite Architecture:** Multiprocessor and its different types, multicomputer, CISC and RISC processors, parallel processing, parallelism in multiprocessor, Flynn’s classification. | 2 | CO3 |
| **Basic Concept:** Introduction to Computer Architecture, Computer Design and Computer Organization. Interaction of hardware with the system and application software, basic hardware components of computer system and bus structure, concept and analysis of performance. | 2 | CO1 |

**Rationale:** This course equips students with a foundational understanding of business formation, industrial operations, and management principles, enabling them to appreciate how organizations are structured and effectively managed. Students develop technical competency in core accounting principles, transaction recording, and the preparation of essential financial documents such as journals, ledgers, trial balances, and worksheets. An introduction to cost terms and classifications further bridges financial and managerial accounting, preparing students to analyze cost-related data and make informed decisions in professional business settings.

| CO2 | Interpret the knowledge of management, plant layout and utility | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 |  WK4 |  |  |
| CO3 | Practice the accounting terms like journals, ledgers, work sheet etc. in real life problem. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO5 |  WK6 |  |  |
| CO4 | Describe the cost terms with classifications. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO2 | WK3 |  |  |

|  **Course Title:** Matrix, Linear Algebra, Differential Equation |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** GED |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  MAT 1107 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces students to the fundamentals of Linear Algebra, Complex Variables, and Differential Equations, emphasizing their application in computer engineering. Students will learn matrix operations, vector spaces, eigenvalues, and eigenvectors for computational problems, use complex variables for analyzing waves, signals, and Fourier transforms, and solve ordinary and partial differential equations to model and simulate engineering and computer graphics systems. The course equips students with mathematical tools essential for problem-solving in graphics, image processing, cryptography, machine learning, and signal processing.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Understand the terms of matrix algebra or solving a general system of linear equations. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO2 | Understand the mathematical problems of functions or limits or continuity for complex variables. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO3 | Solve ordinary and partial differential equations | Cognitive/ Analize | PO2 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO4 | Apply the special function to solve various ODE or PDE equations | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK2 |  |  |

|  **Course Title:** Object Oriented Design and Design Patterns |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2101 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides a structured foundation in Object-Oriented Design (OOD) and Design Patterns, enabling students to model, design, and develop scalable, maintainable, and reusable software systems. It emphasizes core object-oriented principles such as abstraction, encapsulation, inheritance, and polymorphism, and demonstrates how design patterns offer proven solutions to recurring software design problems. By integrating UML-based modeling, real-world case studies, and pattern-oriented thinking, the course enhances students’ ability to translate requirements into robust software architectures, improve code quality, and apply industry-standard best practices essential for modern software development.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Explain fundamental object-oriented design principles and modeling concepts used in developing structured software systems. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO2 | Apply appropriate design patterns to solve common software design problems and improve system reusability and maintainability. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO3 | Analyze and design object-oriented software architectures using UML and pattern-oriented approaches to meet given requirements. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO3 | WK5 |  |  |

| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **OOP Concepts:** Software crisis, need for OOP, objects and classes, object interaction, procedural vs object-oriented approach, Abstraction, encapsulation, inheritance, polymorphism, cohesion and coupling | 4 | CO1 |
| **Advanced OOP:** Interfaces, abstract classes, object relationships, packages, exception handling, SOLID principles (overview), Object responsibility, design heuristics, refactoring, code reusability, maintainability | 3 | CO1, CO2 |
| **Software Design with UML:** Introduction to UML. Overview of the design process using UML Rational Unified Process. Structured description of system usage and function, requirements capture techniques, identification of scenarios and UML use case diagrams. Description of scenario elements in terms of CRC models \- classes, responsibilities and collaborators. Systematic definition of classes, data and system structure. Abstraction, modularization and UML class diagrams. Combining top-down refinement with bottom-up construction. Object interaction, behavior and state. Different varieties of UML interaction diagram. Recognizing coupling between modules and responsibility-driven design. Structuring source code as a design model. Recognition of variable roles. Defensive programming methods including recognition of pre- and post- conditions, invariants, composition and assertions. Rapid and agile prototyping strategies, optimization and debugging techniques | 3 | CO3 |
| **Design Patterns:** Introduction, creational patterns \- abstract factory, builder, factory method, prototype, singleton; structural patterns \- adapter, bridge, composite, decoder, proxy, facade, flyweight; behavioral patterns- chain, command, interpreter, iterator, mediator, memento, observer, state, strategy, visitor, and template method | 4 | CO2, CO3 |

| COs | Instructional Strategy | Assessment Strategy |

| CO1 | Lecture, Practice, and Discussion | Class Test, Midterm Exam |
| CO2 | Lecture, Practice, and Discussion | Class Test, Final Exam |
| CO3 | Lecture, Practice, and Discussion | Assignment, Final Exam |

**Rationale:** This sessional course complements the Object-Oriented Design and Design Patterns theory by providing hands-on experience in applying object-oriented principles, UML-based design, and design patterns to real programming problems. Through structured laboratory exercises and mini-projects, students practice designing classes, modeling systems, implementing reusable and maintainable code, and applying standard design patterns to solve recurring software design issues. The course strengthens problem-solving skills, reinforces theoretical concepts through implementation, and prepares students to develop well-structured, scalable software systems using industry-relevant object-oriented design practices.

|  Course Code:  CSE 2203 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |

|  **Course Title:** Algorithm Analysis and Design |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2103 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course is designed to develop students' ability to design, analyze, and apply efficient algorithms to real-world computational problems. By exploring well-known algorithmic strategies alongside emerging problem-solving techniques, students build the analytical skills needed to evaluate runtime and memory complexity and select the most appropriate solutions. The course further prepares students to tackle graph and combinatorial optimization problems commonly encountered in operations research, while an introduction to computational complexity equips them to assess and classify the inherent difficulty of algorithmic challenges in computer science.  
**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Apply runtime analysis techniques | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO2 | Design necessary algorithms to solve problems in real life. | Cognitive/ Design | PO1 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO3 | Analyze the requirements for approximation of Complexity Classes. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Basics of Algorithms:** Analyzing algorithms, different design techniques, asymptotic notations, best case, worst case and average case time complexity analysis | 1 | CO1 |
| **Sorting Algorithms:** Algorithm and complexity for bubble sort, insertion sort, radix sort, quick sort, merge sort, Topological sort. | 2 | CO1 |
| **Divide and Conquer Design:** Definition, general technique, recurrence relations, and polynomial multiplication, maximum sub array sum problem, analysis of divide and conquer algorithms. | 2 | CO2 |
| **Dynamic Programming:** general principles, difference between divide and conquer and dynamic programming, rod cutting problem, memoization vs. caching, discrete knapsack, longest common subsequence, weighted activity scheduling problem | 2 | CO2 |
| **Greedy Design:** General principles, difference between dynamic programming and greedy, unweighted activity scheduling, fractional knapsack, Huffman coding and their analysis. | 2 | CO2 |
| **Graph Theory:** Single source shortest path for general weight case, and all pair shortest path problem: Floyd Warshall algorithm, network flow problem: Edmond-Karp's algorithm. Matching problem. | 2 | CO2 |
| **String Processing:** Naïve string matching and its pitfalls, KMP algorithm, Trie, Aho-Korasick algorithm, Boye-Moore algorithm. | 1 | CO2 |
| **Computational Complexity:** Definition, polynomial, pseudo polynomial and exponential class, P vs. NP, encoding, reductions, approach to proving NP-completeness, approximation techniques. | 2 | CO3 |

**Rationale:** This course provides students with hands-on experience in implementing a variety of algorithms while leveraging previously learned data structures to enhance computational efficiency. Through practical application of algorithm design techniques, students develop the ability to analyze and solve new problems, examining the trade-offs involved in selecting appropriate data structures for different implementations. The course further cultivates an understanding of the strengths and limitations of various algorithms, while reinforcing good coding practices to prepare students for implementing sophisticated algorithmic solutions in real-world computing environments.

| CO3 | Solve real life problems using the knowledge of algorithms | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK4 | WP3 | EA5 |

|  **Prerequisite(s):**  None |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides students with a comprehensive foundation in computer networking concepts, architectures, and protocols, equipping them with the essential knowledge needed to understand how modern networks are designed and operate. By exploring core networking methodologies and familiarizing students with industry-relevant tools and techniques, the course bridges theoretical understanding with practical application. Upon completion, students are prepared to diagnose network issues and design various types of computer networks, laying the groundwork for advanced study and professional practice in the ever-evolving field of network technology.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Describe various network reference models. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |   |   |
| CO2 | Explain various devices for interconnecting different network layers. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |   |   |
| CO3 | Demonstrate Networking protocols and standards. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK4 | WP1 | EA3 |
| CO4 | Design networks & sub networks. | Cognitive/ Create | PO3 | WK5 | WP6 | EA3 |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO4 |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Introduction:** Computer Networks and Applications, OSI reference model, TCP/IP model and terminology, Connectionless and Connection Oriented services, Service primitives, The ARPANET |  2 | CO1 |
| **Physical Layer:** Circuit switching and Packet switching, X-25 protocol, Frame relay and Cell relay, ATM reference model; Medium Access Sub layer: Pure and slotted ALOHA, Persistent and Non persistent CSMA, CSMA with collision detection and collision free protocols, IEEE standard 802.3 and Ethernet; Data Link Layer: Types of errors, framing, error detection & correction methods; Flow control, Stop & wait ARQ, Go-Back- N ARQ, Selective repeat ARQ, HDLC |  3 |  CO2 |
| **Network Layer:** Internet address, classful address, subnetting, static vs. dynamic routing, shortest path algorithm, flooding, distance vector routing, link state routing, ARP, RARP, IP, ICMP; |  3 |  CO4 |
| **Transport Layer:** UDP, TCP, Connection management, Addressing, Establishing and Releasing Connection, Congestion control algorithm, Flow control and Buffering, Multiplexing; Presentation Layer: Data Compression techniques, Frequency Dependent Coding, Context Dependent Encoding; Application Layer: Internet and intranets, Internet services and goals, DNS, SMTP, FTP, Telnet, HTTP, World Wide Web (WWW), DHCP and BOOTP; |  3 |  CO3 |
| **Networking in Practice:** Designing LAN, Cabling, Establishing Client- Server network, Configuring: Directory Server, Proxy server, FTP server, E-mail server, web server, DB server, Firewall, Network troubleshooting, network maintenance, network monitoring, Network programming |  3 |  CO2 |

|  **Course Status:** GED |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  MAT 1107, MAT 2109 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides students with a solid foundation in the fundamental concepts of statistics and probability, tailored to the analytical demands of computer engineering. By covering essential topics such as measures of central tendency and dispersion, correlation, regression, sampling techniques, and probability distributions, students acquire the quantitative tools necessary to interpret and analyze statistical data effectively. The course further develops students' ability to construct meaningful graphical representations of data, equipping them with the practical statistical skills required to address real-world engineering problems and support informed decision-making in professional and research contexts.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Apply central tendency or dispersion. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO1 | WK1 |  |  |
| CO2 | Construct a frequency distribution table or construct statistical charts. | Psychomotor/ Practicing | PO2 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO3 | Solve the problem of correlation or regression or probability. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK3 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **An introduction:** Definition of Statistics, Uses and importance, Variables, Measurement level of variables, Sample and Population. **Measures of central tendency:** Introduction, the arithmetic mean, Geometric mean, Harmonic mean, Median, mode, computing of means for grouped data and ungrouped data, Advantages, and disadvantages, and Uses of all measures. **Measures of dispersion:** Introduction of dispersion, Absolute and Relative measures, Range, mean deviation, Standard deviation, Variance, Quartile deviation, Coefficient of Variation, Advantages, disadvantages and uses of all measures. | 5 | CO1 |
| **Data representation:** Classification, Types of classification, Tabular representation, Graphical representation, Pie chart, Histogram, Bar chart, Frequency curve, Frequency Polygon and Ogive curve. | 2 | CO2 |
| **Correlation and Regression:** Correlation, Types of correlation, Importance and uses of correlation, Properties of correlation, Rank correlation, Regression, Differences between regression line and regression model, Simple linear Regression, Importance of regression analysis, Comparison of regression and correlation. **Probability:** Concept of probability, Sample Space, Events, Union and Intersection of Events, Probability of events, Laws of probability, Conditional probabilities, Bayes’ theorem, Chebyshev’s Inequality and Practical examples. **Random variables and probability Distribution:** Basic concepts, Discrete and continuous random variables, Probability Mass Function (PMF), Probability Density Function (PDF), Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF), Mathematical expectation and variance, Joint, marginal and conditional density functions, Conditional Expectation and conditional variance, Probability generating function, Characteristic function, Binomial distribution, Poisson distribution, and Normal distribution with Practical examples. **Sampling design and Statistical test:** Sampling Design, Simple random sampling, Systematic sampling, Stratified sampling; Cluster sampling, Judgment sampling, Chi-squared test, Z test, t-test and F-test. | 7 | CO3 |

**Rationale:** Engineering Economics introduces students to economic principles and analytical techniques required for evaluating engineering projects and decision-making under limited resources. The course emphasizes time value of money, cost estimation, cash flow analysis, depreciation, and project evaluation methods. Students develop the ability to compare alternatives, assess financial feasibility, and make informed economic decisions in engineering practice.

| CO3 | Correlate the concept of various sections of national income with the budgets of Bangladesh. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO7 | WK7 |  |  |

|  Course Code:  CSE 3101 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Database Management System |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2103 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** The course on Database Management System (DBMS) is designed to equip students with both theoretical foundations and practical skills essential for managing and manipulating structured data in modern computing environments. Students will gain comprehensive knowledge of core database concepts and hands-on experience with Data Definition Language (DDL), Data Manipulation Language (DML), and Data Control Language (DCL) to define, manipulate, and secure databases effectively. The course emphasizes relational database design through Entity-Relationship (ER) diagrams and integrity constraints, enabling students to model real-world data accurately using SQL-based RDBMS. Furthermore, students will explore storage and file structures, as well as transaction processing systems, fostering a deeper understanding of how databases operate at both logical and physical levels, thereby preparing them for roles in software development, data engineering, and system administration.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Explain the fundamental concepts and terminologies associated with database management systems. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO2 | Design and implement a relational database model applying normalization and structured query techniques | Cognitive/ Apply | PO3 | WK5 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply normalization techniques to minimize data redundancy and ensure data integrity in relational database design. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO5 | WK6 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Introduction:** Data and information, Purpose of Database Systems, View of Data, Database Languages DDL, DML, DCL. **Database Design and the E-R Diagram:** Entity and attributes, Entity-Relationship Model, Entity-Relationship Diagrams, convert E-R model into tables. **Relational Data Model:** Structure of Relational Databases, Database Schema, Keys, Schema, Diagrams, Relational Query Languages, Relational Database Design, Normalizing database up to 3rd normal form, BCNF. | 5 | CO1, CO2 |
| **SQL:** Overview of the SQL Query, Language, SQL Data Definition, Basic Structure of SQL, Queries, Aggregate Functions, Modification of the Database, Join Expressions, Views, Transactions, Integrity Constraints, Functions and Procedures, Triggers, Recursive Queries, Advanced Aggregation Features. | 4 | CO1, CO3 |
| **Storage and File Structure:** Overview of Physical Storage Media, Magnetic Disk and Flash Storage, RAID, Tertiary Storage, File Organization, Organization of Records in Files, Data-Dictionary Storage, Database Buffer. **Transactions:** Transaction Concept, A Simple Transaction Model, Storage Structure, Transaction Atomicity and Durability, Transaction Isolation, Transaction Isolation and Atomicity, Transaction Isolation Levels, Implementation of Isolation Levels, Transactions as SQL Statements, Lock-Based Protocols, Deadlock Handling, Multiple Granularity, Timestamp-Based Protocols, Validation-Based Protocols. Database-System Architectures: Centralized and Client–Server Architectures, Server System Architectures, Parallel Systems, Design and types of Parallel Systems, Distributed Systems architecture and its application. | 5 | CO1 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 3102 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Database Management System Sessional |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Sessional |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2104 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** The course provides students with hands-on experience in designing and implementing relational database systems through structured laboratory exercises. Students will practice writing and executing Data Definition Language (DDL) and Data Manipulation Language (DML) commands to create, modify, and manage database objects effectively. The lab sessions are specifically designed to develop students' ability to perform indexing, hashing, and cascading operations, alongside the application of integrity constraints and normalization techniques to identify and eliminate data anomalies, ensuring a well-structured and consistent database. Additionally, students will gain practical exposure to stored procedures and database functions, enabling them to build modular and reusable database solutions for real-world problem domains, thereby bridging the gap between theoretical knowledge and industry-level database application development.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Design and implement a normalized relational database schema for a given real-world problem domain.  | Cognitive/ Create | PO5 | WK6 |   |   |
| CO2 | Execute SQL-based DDL and DML commands to create, manipulate, and manage database structures and records.  | Cognitive/ Apply | PO1 | WK3 | WP1   | EA3 |
| CO3 | Develop stored procedures and user-defined functions to provide modular and efficient solutions for domain-specific database problems.  | Cognitive/ Apply | PO3 | WK5 | WP5 | EA5 |

| Create a relational database for a domain like an organization and discuss how the schema is effective for that organization. Design an E-R diagram and mapping a relational model from that E-R diagram for a given scenario. | 5 | CO1 |
| Practice intermediate and advanced SQL operation for a given relation. Intermediate SQL: Join Expressions, Views etc. Advanced SQL: Trigger, Stored procedure, sub query etc. | 5 | CO2 |
| Practice cascading operations using keys for the given relations. Removing insertion, deletion and update anomalies using normalization technique and redesign the database schema. | 4 | CO3 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 3103 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |

|  **Course Title:** Computer Graphics |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  MAT 2109 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** ThIs course equips students with foundational and applied knowledge of visualization techniques, covering the RGB color model, graphics display systems, and image processing concepts. Students learn core rendering algorithms including point plotting, line and circle drawing, and polygon filling, progressing to complex shape construction, 2D/3D transformations, viewing, clipping, and curve and surface fitting techniques. .

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Explain the algorithmic interpretation for scan conversion of basic geometric shapes.  | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO2 | Apply mathematical formulations to perform 2D and 3D geometric transformations. . | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO3 | Compare the efficiency and accuracy of different curve and line drawing algorithms.  | Cognitive/ Evaluate | PO4 | WK8 |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Scan Conversion:** Points plotting techniques, Line drawing algorithms: Horizontal and vertical line drawing algorithm, Direct Line drawing algorithm, DDA algorithm, Bresenham’s algorithm. Circle Drawing algorithms: Direct method, Mid-point circle drawing algorithm. Ellipse generating algorithm. | 4 | CO1 |
| **Polygon Filling:** Scan line polygon fill algorithm, Boundary fill algorithm and Flood fill algorithm. | 2 | CO1 |
| **2D and 3D transformation:** Basic transformations (2D and 3D): Translation, Scaling, Rotation, Other transformations (2D and 3D): Reflection, Reflection through an arbitrary plane, Rotation about an arbitrary axis in space, shearing. Matrix representations and Homogeneous coordinates, General parallel projection Transformation. | 4 | CO2 |
| **Viewing and Clipping:** Viewing pipeline, Window to viewport coordinate transformation, 3D viewing, View port clipping, clipping operations: point clipping, line clipping, polygon clipping etc. | 2 | CO2, CO3 |
| **Curves:** Curve representation, Surfaces design, Hermit curves, Bezier curves, B-spline curves, End conditions for periodic B-spline curves, Rational B-spline curves. | 2 | CO3 |

|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2105 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** The Microprocessor and Assembly Language course introduces students to the architecture and low-level programming of the Intel 8086 microprocessor, providing a thorough understanding of its operation at the address, data, and control levels. Students gain knowledge of instruction sets, memory addressing modes, and flow control mechanisms, and develop the ability to write and analyze assembly language programs to solve computational problems using logic, shift, and rotation operations.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Describe the organization and instruction set architecture of the Intel 8086 microprocessor. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO2 | Explain various memory addressing modes and their application in assembly language programming.  | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply flow control, shift, and rotation instructions in assembly language programs.  | Cognitive/ Apply | PO1 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO4 | Solve computational problems using logic operations in 8086 assembly language.  | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK2 |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides students with hands-on experience in 8086 assembly language programming through structured laboratory exercises. Students practice writing and executing programs involving basic input/output operations, arithmetic and logic operations, and decision-making instructions, developing practical problem-solving skills at the hardware-software interface level.

|  Course Code: CSE 3107 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Theory of Computation and Compiler Design |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2203 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course equips students with foundational knowledge of formal languages, automata models, and computational theory, covering regular languages, expressions, and decision problems. Students also gain comprehensive knowledge of compiler construction, including parsing techniques, the phases of compilation, code optimization strategies, and error handling mechanisms, preparing them to understand and design efficient language processing systems. 

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Describe the structure and operation of finite automata and its application in computational decision making.  | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO2 | Apply appropriate automata models to recognize and process regular languages and expressions.  | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO3 | Discuss the distinct phases of a compiler and their roles in program translation.  | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO4 | Analyze code optimization schemes to improve the efficiency of compiler design.  | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO4 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Languages and Grammars:** Finite Automata \- accepting languages, strings, string search algorithm, distinguishing strings, integers, lexical analysis, decision problems and languages, minimizing finite automata. | 4 | 1 |
| **Regular languages and Expression:** Non-deterministic finite automata, Kleene's theorem. Context-free languages, regular languages and grammars. Simplified forms and normal forms. Push-Down	Automata-	deterministic	PDA    and	non-deterministic PDA, top-down and bottom-up PDA, Parsing \- top down and bottom-up parsers. Decision problems and CFL. **Computational Models:** Computational tasks \- search and decision problems, General model of computation, Turing Machines. **Decision Problems:** Undecidable problems, reduction and halting problem, context-free languages. Decision trees. Satisfiability problem. | 5 | 2 |
| **Introduction:** Introduction to compiler, compiler and translator, the structure of a compiler. **Parsing:** Basic parsing technique, parsers, shift reduce parsing, operator procedure parsing, top-down parsing, bottom-up parsing, predictive parsing. **Syntax:** Syntax directed translation, intermediate code generation, polish notation, parse tree and syntax trees, quadruples, triples, Boolean expression. **Symbol Table:** Perspective and motivation of symbol table. Symbol table content, operation on symbol table, organization of symbol table. | 3 | 3 |
| **Code Optimization:** Code optimization, sources of optimization, basic blocks, folding, loop optimization, flowgraph, induction variable elimination, reduction in strength, code motion. Code generation, object programs, problems in code generation, a machine model, a simple code generator, register allocation. | 2 | 4 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 3109 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |

|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  MAT 2109 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces the basic concepts and methodologies of numerical methods. The covered topics include Floating-point arithmetic, Error analysis, Root finding, Systems of linear algebraic equations, Matrix eigenvalue problems, Curve Fitting, Numerical Differentiation and Integration and ordinary differential equations. It also familiarizes techniques and concepts of numerical methods. The motivation of the course is to develop basics knowledge on numerical methods.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Discuss the schemes to estimate the approximation and error term. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO2 | Describe different methods to solve system of equations and single equation | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply appropriate techniques for approximating functions | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO4 | Evaluate the performance of different numerical techniques to solve differential equations | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO3 | WK5 |  |  |

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| Floating-point representations, General properties, Floating- point exception handling, Rounding methods, Floating-point operations (+, \-, ×, /), Accuracy and Precision, Error Definitions, Round-Off Errors, Truncation Errors. | 2 | CO1 |
| Graphical Methods, The Bisection Method, The False- Position Method, SimpleOne-Point Iteration, The Newton- Raphson Method, The Ramanujan’s Method,The Secant Method. Gauss Elimination, Solving Small Numbers of Equations, Naive Gauss Elimination, Pitfalls of Elimination Methods, Matrix Inversion and Gauss – Seidel, The Matrix Inverse. Singular Value Decomposition | 4 | CO2 |
| Polynomial Interpolation, Newton’s Interpolation, Gauss’s central interpolation, Lagrange interpolation. Linear Regression, Polynomial Regression, Multiple Linear Regression, least square curve fitting procedure, Weighted least square approximation. | 4 | CO3 |
| The Trapezoidal Rule, Simpson’s Rules, Integration with Unequal Segments, High-Accuracy Differentiation Formulas, Derivatives of Unequally Spaced Data, Ordinary and partial differential equations, Numerical solution techniques for ordinary differential equation, error analysis of the ODE solution. | 4 | CO4 |

## **3rd Year 2nd Semester**

|  Course Code:  CSE 3201 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Operating System |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2105 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces students to the fundamental concepts, design principles, and implementation strategies of modern operating systems for both single-user and multi-user environments. Students gain comprehensive knowledge of computer system structure, OS services, system calls, process management, CPU scheduling, inter-process communication, process synchronization, and deadlock handling. The course further covers memory management techniques including hardware address protection, swapping, virtual memory, caching, and file system management, providing students with the analytical skills needed to evaluate and support operating system functions in real-world computing environments.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Explain the core concepts, structure, and functionalities of modern operating systems. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO2 | Evaluate and compare the efficiency of various CPU scheduling algorithms. | Cognitive/ Evaluate | PO4 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO3 | Describe the mechanisms of process synchronization and strategies for deadlock detection and management. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO4 | Illustrate the principles and techniques of memory management systems including swapping and virtual memory. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | ----- | :---: |
| Introduction to operating systems, OS services and structures, system calls, types of operating systems | Weeks 1–3 | CO1 |
| Process concept, process states, CPU scheduling algorithms, inter-process communication, synchronization mechanisms | Weeks 4–7 | CO2 |
| Deadlocks (characterization, prevention, avoidance), memory management, paging, segmentation, virtual memory | Weeks 8–11 | CO3 |
| File systems, disk scheduling, I/O systems, protection and security, performance evaluation, course review | Weeks 8–11 | CO4 |

|  **Course Title:** Operating System Sessional |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Sessional |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2106 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides students with practical experience in working with open-source operating systems through virtual machine environments, using Ubuntu Linux as the primary platform. Students learn to navigate and operate the system via terminal commands, implement system calls, and manage processes, progressively advancing to the hands-on implementation and performance evaluation of CPU scheduling algorithms including FCFS, SJF, Round Robin, and Priority Scheduling, along with kernel compilation, bridging theoretical OS concepts with real-world system-level programming skills.

| CO2 | Apply process management concepts including system calls and process creation in a Linux environment. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO1 | WK4 | WP1 | EA1 |
| CO3 | Evaluate and compare the performance of various CPU scheduling algorithms through practical implementation. | Cognitive/ Evaluate | PO4 | WK8 | WP3 | EA3 |

|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2101 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces students to both fundamental and advanced concepts of web application development and online business systems. Students gain practical skills in front-end development using HTML, CSS, and JavaScript, alongside back-end programming with PHP and database integration. The course further covers advanced web technologies including AJAX and multimedia integration, with emphasis on real-world e-commerce functionalities such as form processing, session management, and secure database interaction, preparing students for professional roles in web development and online business environments.

| CO2 | Discuss client-side and server-side web programming techniques for dynamic web application development. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO3 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO3 | Analyze the architecture and functionalities of database-driven e-commerce web applications. | Cognitive/ Create | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |

**Rationale:** This sessional course is designed to provide hands-on experience in developing real-world web and e-commerce applications. It reinforces the theoretical concepts taught in the E-commerce and Web Programming course through practical implementation using modern web technologies.

Students will design and implement interactive web applications using HTML, CSS, JavaScript, PHP, and database systems. Emphasis is placed on project-based learning, where students integrate front-end and back-end technologies to build a complete e-commerce system. The course develops practical skills in problem solving, system integration, and professional web development practices.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| CO2 | Develop server-side web applications with database integration | Cognitive/ Create | PO5 | WK6 | WP6 | EA3 |
| CO3 | Develop a complete e-commerce project integrating front-end, back-end, and database systems. | Cognitive/ Create | PO12 |   |   |   |

|  Course Code:  CSE 3205 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
