|  **Course Title:** Digital Signal Processing |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  MAT 2109, CSE 3109 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces students to the fundamental concepts and methodologies of analyzing and processing digital signals. Students gain knowledge of signal discretization, z-transforms, frequency analysis, and the Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT), progressing to the design and implementation of digital filters including FIR and IIR filters. The course develops a strong analytical foundation in signal processing techniques, equipping students with the skills to analyze system responses and apply frequency domain methods for real-world signal processing applications. 

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Describe the fundamental concepts of signals, systems, and the discretization process.  | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO2 | Analyze the response of Linear Time-Invariant (LTI) systems to various input signals.  | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO3 | Illustrate the concepts and applications of frequency domain signal processing using DFT and z-transform.  | Cognitive/ Understand | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO4 | Apply Fourier transform techniques to design and implement frequency domain digital filters.  | Cognitive/ Apply | PO3 | WK5 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO4 |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| Introduction to DSP: Motivation and real-time applications of DSP; Basic concepts and classification of signals; Analog and discrete signals; Discrete-time signal types and operations; Applications of discrete-time signals; Analog to Digital Conversion — Sampling and Quantization; Digital to Analog Conversion | 3 | CO1 |
| Discrete-Time Signals and Systems: Discrete-time signals and system analysis; Linear Time-Invariant (LTI) system analysis; Discrete-time systems described by difference equations; Implementation of discrete-time systems; Correlation and convolution of discrete-time signals | 4 | CO2 |
| Discrete Fourier Transform and FFT: The DFT and its properties; Filtering methods based on DFT; Frequency analysis of signals using DFT; FFT algorithms and their derivation; Applications of FFT in signal processing | 3 | CO3 |
| Digital Filter Design: Design and implementation of FIR filters; Design and implementation of IIR filters; Comparison of FIR and IIR filter characteristics | 2 | CO3, CO4 |
| Adaptive Filters: Adaptive systems and concepts; Kalman filters; RLS adaptive filters; Steepest-descent method; LMS adaptive filters and applications | 2 | CO4 |

| CO2 | Lecture, Practice, and Discussion | Class Test, Midterm Exam |
| CO3 | Lecture, Practice, and Discussion | Assignment, Final Exam |
| CO4 | Lecture, Practice, and Discussion | Assignment, Final Exam |

**Rationale:** This course provides students with hands-on experience in implementing signal processing techniques for both analog and digital signals using structured laboratory exercises. Assignments progressively advance from fundamental signal manipulation including amplitude and time modifications to statistical feature extraction and frequency domain transformations and filtering, enabling students to apply theoretical signal processing concepts to solve real-world problems effectively. 

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Demonstrate amplitude and time domain modifications on discrete-time signals. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO1 | WK3 | WP1 | EA1 |
| CO2 | Compute and interpret statistical features extracted from real-world signals. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK2 |  WP2 |   |
| CO3 | Implement frequency domain transformations and filtering techniques on discrete signals. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO4 | WK8 |  WP2 | EA3 |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| Introduction & Basic Signal Generation: Overview of MATLAB/Python software environment; Generating waveforms — amplitude and frequency control; Generation of continuous and discrete basic signals (sine, cosine, unit step, impulse, ramp); Signal addition and multiplication operations | Weeks 1 – 3 | CO1  |
| Signal Operations: Time shifting operations on discrete signals; Folding and time reversal operations; Mixed problems involving different types of signal operations; Amplitude and time modification exercises | Weeks 4 – 6 | CO1  |
| Real-World Signal Analysis & Feature Extraction: Shape modification of real-world signals with noise; Noise addition and its effect on signal waveform; Extracting statistical features of signals — mean, variance, energy, power, SNR; Signal visualization and interpretation | Weeks 7 – 9 | CO2 |
| Fourier Transform & Frequency Domain Analysis: Introduction to Fourier Transform and its implementation; DFT and FFT computation on discrete signals; Frequency spectrum visualization and analysis; Inverse Fourier Transform and signal reconstruction | Weeks 10 –  12 | CO3 |
| Convolution & Filtering: Convolution of discrete-time signals — implementation and visualization; LTI system response using convolution; Frequency domain filtering on discrete signals; Performance evaluation and real-world signal filtering | Weeks 13 – 14 | CO2, CO3 |

|  Course Code: CSE 3207 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |

|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2201 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces the basics of software engineering, software development life-cycles. SDLC models, design of solution of a problem partition are also learned. They can learn about testing and software project management. They can estimate the cost using techniques, schedule a project, and manage the risks. They can also learn about system concepts and the information system environment, the role of system analyst, analysis information Gathering, cost/benefit analysis. They can design system design and implement with testing and quality assurance.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Interpret the various industry standard software development models and utilize different design techniques | Cognitive/ Understand | PO9 |   |   |   |
| CO2 | Demonstrate various software testing methods. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | K4 | P1 | A1 |
| CO3 | Demonstrate the project management scheme in software engineering. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO11 |   | P6 | A3 |
| CO4 | Identify the principles and tools of systems analysis and design and design a candidate system with consideration of professional. | Cognitive/ Create | PO8 | K7 | P7 | A5 |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Software Development Life-Cycle:** Requirement analysis, Software design, Coding, Testing and Maintenance, SDLC models like Water fall model, Incremental model, Spiral model, Agile model and their role of management in software development. |   1 |   CO1 |
| **Design:** Problem partitioning, Top-down and bottom-up design, Functional versus object-oriented approach, Component-level design, User-system interaction, Data visualization etc. | 1 | CO1 |
| **Software Project Management:** Cost estimation and techniques, Project scheduling, Risk management, Role of matrices and measurement. | 2 | CO3 |
| **Software Reliability:** Reliability growth modelling, ISO 9000 certification for software industry, SEI capability maturity model, Comparison between ISO & SEI CMM. | 2 | CO3 |
| **Testing:** Verification and validation, testing strategies, Unit testing, Integration testing, Top-down and bottom-up testing, Alpha and Beta testing, System testing and debugging. | 2 | CO2 |
| **Overview:** System concepts and the information system environment, The system development life cycle, The role of systems analyst and the concepts of MIS. | 2 | CO4 |
| **System Analysis:** Information Gathering, The tools of structured analysis, Cost/benefit analysis. | 2 | CO4 |
| **System Design and Implementation:** The process and stages of system design, Input/output and forms design, System testing and quality assurance. | 2 | CO4 |

|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces students to the fundamental principles and modern advancements in communication systems, covering analog and digital communication, optical fiber communication, satellite and cellular communication, and emerging 5G technologies. Students develop a thorough understanding of modulation techniques, signal transmission, and system components including source, channel, and receiver, equipping them with the analytical knowledge and research foundation needed to keep pace with rapidly evolving communication technologies.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Describe the fundamental terminologies and concepts of communication engineering systems. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO2 | Analyze analog and digital modulation techniques and discuss the components of a digital communication system. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO3 | Explain optical fiber, satellite, and cellular communication systems including emerging 5G technologies.  | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Communication Engineering Fundamentals**, Waveforms Spectra, Elements of basic communication systems, Periodic and nonperiodic waveforms and their properties, Fourier series, Noise and its different types. | 2 | CO1 |
| **Analogue Communication:** Amplitude modulation, Amplitude modulation index, Frequency spectrum for sinusoidal AM, Frequency Modulation: Frequency Modulation, Sinusoidal FM, Frequency spectrum for Sinusoidal FM, Phase Modulation. Pulse Modulation, Pulse Codes Modulation (PCM), Quantization, Compression, PCM Receiver, Differential PCM, Delta Modulation, Sigma-Delta A/D conversion, Pulse Modulation. Analog Multiplexing **Digital Communication:** Basic Digital Communication System, Synchronization, Asynchronous Transmission, Probability of Bit Error in Base band Transmission, Matched Filter, Eye Diagrams, Digital Carrier Systems, Amplitude Shift keying, Frequency Shift Keying, Phase Shift Keying, Carrier Recovery Circuits, Quadrature Phase Shift Keying, Error Control Coding, Block Control, Repetition Encoding, Parity Encoding, Convolution Encoding. Digital Multiplexing. | 10 | CO2 |
| **Satellite Communication:** Satellite Communication, Kepler’s First and Second Law, Orbits, Geostationary Orbits, Power System. 5G technologies. **Fiber Optic Communication:** Fiber Optic Communication, Propagation within a Fiber, Modes of Propagation, Losses in Fibers, Light sources for Fiber optics, Photo detectors. | 2 | CO3 |

**Rationale:** This course familiarizes students with the fundamentals of professional report writing and oral communication skills essential for academic and industry environments. |  Course Code:  CSE 4101 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |

|  **Course Title:** Digital Image Processing |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 3205 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces the basic concepts and methodologies of digital signal processing. The covered topics include signals, systems and signal processing, The z-transform, frequency analysis of signals and systems, the Discrete Fourier Transform: The DFT and Digital Filters: Design of FIR and IIR filters. It also familiarizes signal analysis techniques and concepts. The motivation of the course is to develop basics knowledge on Digital Signal Processing.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Describe concepts of signal and its discretization process. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO2 | Analyze the response of linear time invariant (LTI) systems to any input signal. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO3 | Illustrate the concept and application of frequency domain signal processing | Cognitive/ Understand | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO4 | Apply Fourier transform to implement frequency domain filtering. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO3 | WK5 |  |  |

Different types of discrete time signals, applications of discrete time signals, different operations of Signals. Analog to Digital Conversion, Sampling, Quantization. Digital to Analog Conversion. | 3 | CO1 |

|  **Course Status:** GED |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  None |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** The course aims to provide an understanding of the principles of Engineering Ethics and Environmental Protection. As an Engineer, it is one of the utmost duties is maintain a strong morality so that the things they create are not used for harmful purpose, and also ensure that the inventions or products made do not have a bad impact on the environment.  

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Correlate between the attributes of engineers with ethical conundrums | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO8 | WK7 |  |  |
| CO2 | Illustrate and demonstrate the responsibilities towards the society and the environment | Cognitive/ Apply | PO7 | WK7 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply ethical knowledge of engineers in the protection of the environment | Cognitive/ Apply | PO7 | WK7 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 |  |  |  |  |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  |  |  |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  |  |  |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Introduction to Engineering ethics and professionalism:** What is engineering ethics? Why study engineering ethics? Responsible Professionals, Professions, and Corporations, The Origins of Ethical Thought, Ethics and the Law, Moral Reasoning and Codes of Ethics: Ethical decision-making strategies, Ethical dilemmas, Codes of ethics, Case studies **Moral Frameworks for Engineering Ethics:** Ethical theories, Personal commitments, and professional life **Ethical Problem-Solving Techniques:** Analysis of Issues in Ethical Problems, An Application of Problem-Solving Methods, Engineering as Social Experimentation: Engineering as Experimentation, Engineers as Responsible Experimenters, Risk, Safety, and Accidents: Assessment of safety and risk, Design considerations, uncertainty, Risk-benefit analysis, safe-exit and fail- safe systems | 5 | CO1 |
| **Engineer's Responsibilities and Rights:** Employee/employer rights and responsibilities, Confidentiality and conflict of interest, Whistleblowing, Case studies on whistleblowing **Honesty and Research Integrity:** Truthfulness, Trustworthiness, Research Integrity, Protecting Research Subjects **Computer Ethics:** The Internet and Free Speech, Power Relationships, Property, Privacy, Additional Issues **Environmental Ethics:** Engineering, ecology, economics, Sustainable development, Ethical frameworks **Global Issues:** Multinational corporations, globalization of engineering, Technology transfer, appropriate technology **Cautious Optimism and Moral Leadership:** Cautious optimism as a technology development attitude, Moral leadership in engineering **Cyber Law:** Definition Nature, Scope, Utility of Cyber Law, Origin and Development of Cyber Law and Internet **ICT Policy in Bangladesh;** Internet Service Providers (ISP)- Domain Name, Present Legal Basis of ISP in Bangladesh; e- Readiness in Bangladesh- e-Commerce in Bangladesh, e-Governance in Bangladesh, e-Learning/Education in Bangladesh, e-Journal in Bangladesh, e-Voting in Bangladesh; Electronic Evidence- Digital Signature, The Evidence Act of 1872 Vs. ICT Act-2006, Electronic Evidence in Bangladesh, Legal Effects of Electronic Evidence, UNCITRAL Model Law on Electronic Evidence; **Cyber Crime:** Jurisdiction and Cyber Crime, Criminal Justice in Bangladesh and Implications on Cyber Crime; Cyber vandalism, Hacking, Malicious Spreading in Viruses, Password fraud, Cheating, Cyber Pornography, Child Pornography, Protection of Copyrights and Intellectual Property right, Invasion of Privacy, Constitutional basis of Privacy, Unsolicited E-Mail, Defamation, Harassment and e-Mail Abuse, Present Legal Protection; **Human Rights Violation and Internet;** The Information and Communication Technology Act, 2006- Objectives, Strengths & Weaknesses of the ICT Law, Regulation of Cryptography; | 5 | CO2 |
| **Environmental Protection Environmental Laws of Bangladesh:** The Environment Conservation Act 1995, The Environment Court Act 2010, The Forest Act 1927, The Wildlife (Conservation and Security) Act 2012, The Brick Manufacturing and Brick Kilns Establishment (Control) Act 2013, The Bangladesh Biodiversity Act 2017 **International Environment Law:** International Environment Law and its effects and regulations on the computer technology industry. | 3 | CO3 |

**Rationale:** This course introduces the fundamental principles of project management and entrepreneurship. The covered topics include project initiation, planning, execution, monitoring, and closing, alongside key entrepreneurial concepts such as opportunity identification, business model development, feasibility analysis, and financing. The goal is to equip students with the skills required to manage engineering projects effectively and to foster an entrepreneurial mindset for creating innovative ventures.

## **Table \- I**

|  Course Code:  CSE 4107 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Machine Learning |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Elective |  |  **Course Type:**   Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  STA 2207 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course introduces the basic concepts and methodologies of machine learning. The covered topics include aspects of developing a learning system, different kind of learning systems, data pre-processing techniques, supervised learning, unsupervised learning, reinforcement learning, ensemble learning, performance metrics of machine learning algorithms and basic concepts of deep learning. The course will be project-oriented, with emphasis placed on implementations of learning algorithms applied to real-world problems. After completing the course, the students will be able to understand the basic concepts of machine learning and build systems that can learn and adapt through real-world applications.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- |
| CO1 | Identify the suitable problem area for application of supervised, unsupervised and reinforcement learning. | Cognitive/ Understand | 2 | K4 |   |  |
| CO2 | Illustrate when regularization can be used. | Cognitive/ Apply | 1 | K4 |   |  |
| CO3 | Analyze the performance of different machine learning algorithms on real data. | Cognitive/ Analyze | 4 | K8 |   |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Fundamentals of Machine Learning:** Definition and types of learning systems, Goals and applications of machine learning, Aspects of developing a learning system, Main steps in a typical machine learning project, Challenges of Machine Learning like Overfitting, Underfitting, Irrelevant Features etc. | 1 | CO1 |
| **Knowing Your Data:** Different Attribute Types, Basic Statistical Descriptions of Data, Measuring Data Similarity and Dissimilarity; Machine Learning Terms: Feature, Train Data, Test Data, Validation Data, Response Variable, Labelled/ Unlabeled Data, partitioning a Dataset Depending on a Target Variable, Cross Validation Method, Optimizing a cost function | 2 | CO3 |
| **Handling and preparing Data:** Data Cleaning, Data Reduction, Data Integration, How to Prepare Data for Machine Learning Algorithm, Reducing the dimensionality of the training data to fight the curse of dimensionality, dimensionality reduction algorithms, Selecting and engineering features, Selecting a model and tuning hyper parameters using cross-validation, facing the main challenges in machine learning- particularly underfitting and overfitting (the bias/variance tradeoff) , Learning by fitting a model to data,  Ensemble learning. | 2 | CO2 |
| **Supervised ML:** Basic Concepts of classification, Multiclass/Multilabel/Multioutput Classification, Decision Tree Induction, Bayes Classiﬁcation Methods, Rule-Based Classiﬁcation, Solving Real Life Problems using Supervised ML Algorithms like SVM, Decision Tree, Naïve Bayes, Linear Regression, Non-Linear Regression, Time-series Analysis. | 2 | CO1 |
| **Unsupervised ML:** What is Cluster Analysis and What are the Requirements, Basic Overview of Clustering Methods like Partitioning/ Hierarchical/ Density-based/ Grid based Method; Solving Real Life Problems Using K-Means Clustering Algorithm and its challenges; | 2 | CO1 |
| **Performance Metrics of Supervised and Unsupervised ML Algorithms:** R-Squared Value, Confusion Matrix, Accuracy, Precision, Recall, F1 measure, Specificity, ROC curve**,** Elbow Method | 2 | CO3 |
| **Introduction to Deep Learning:** Neural nets and for what they are good for, The most important neural net architectures: feedforward neural nets, convolutional nets, recurrent nets, long short-term memory (LSTM) nets, and autoencoders, Techniques for training deep neural nets, Reinforcement learning | 2 | CO1 |

**Rationale:** To provide hands on experience in implementing data analysis, data pre-processing techniques and developing machine learning models capable of providing solutions to real life problems. The laboratory assignments progress from investigation of the basic properties of machine learning to the development and evaluation of machine learning models.

| CO3 | Evaluate the efficiency of different machine learning algorithms on real data. | Cognitive/ Evaluate | PO4 | WK8 | WP4 | EA4 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 4109 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |

|  **Course Title:** Microcontroller, Computer Peripherals and Interfacing |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Elective |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 3105 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** The course discusses the basic concepts of microcontrollers, computer peripherals and interfacing. This course is designed based on the popular Microchip AVR microcontrollers. The covered topics include microcontroller and embedded system introduction, microcontroller I/O port programming, special features of microcontroller, peripheral features of microcontroller, I/O controller and computer peripheral components, interfacing components. After completing the course, the students will have enough knowledge regarding microcontrollers and an understanding of computer peripherals along with the interfacing techniques to work on an embedded system.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Illustrate the brief concepts of microcontroller, microcomputer, and embedded system. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK2 |  |  |
| CO2 | Describe the interfacing techniques of memory and I/O with microprocessor. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK3, |  |  |
| CO3 | Discuss the data communication technique with serial I/O | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |

| CO2 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| Introduction to Microcontroller and Embedded System: Classification and applications, Overview of AVR Family, History and Features, Embedded System Specifications and Modelling, Embedded Hardware Platforms and Peripherals, Embedded Processors, Embedded Computing Platform, Real Time Embedded Systems, Embedded Systems Programming, Embedded Communication Systems, Embedded Computer Security. | 2 | CO1 |
| Microcontroller I/O Port Programming: General Purpose Registers, Port Programming, Bit Manipulation, AVR Hardware Connection, Hex Files | 1 | CO3 |
| Microcontroller   Peripheral   Features:   Timer,   PWM,	Serial Communication Subsystems (USART, UART, SPI, TWI Protocols). | 1 | CO3 |
| Microcontroller Special Features: External and Internal Interrupt, ADC | 1 | CO3 |
| Basic Concepts of Microprocessor Interfacing: Reviews of Address Decoding Concepts, Input and Output Port Design, Decoder, Encoder, Multiplexer, De-multiplexer, Microcomputer System Overview | 2 | CO1 |
| I/O Controller and Computer Peripheral Components: Interfacing ICs of I/O Devices, I/O Ports, Programmable Peripheral Interface, DMA Controller, Interrupt Controller, Communication Interface, Interval Timer, Mapping Between Languages and Hardware | 2 | CO2 |
| Memory Device: Memory Terminology, CPU-Memory Connections, Memory Interfacing ROM Architectures and Time Diagram, Different Type of ROM, Flash Memory, RAM Architectures and Time Diagram, Different Type of RAM and Read/Write Cycle, Programmable Logic Device Architectures | 2 | CO2 |
| Interfacing Components: Programmable Timer, Bus Architecture, Bus Timing, Transceiver Device, Latches, Bus Controller, Hard Disk Controllers, Barcode Reader, Sound Card, Printer Interface, Characteristics of Memory And I/O Interface, Synchronous- Asynchronous communication, Serial I/O Interface, Programmable Peripheral Interface, Real Time Operating Systems | 2 | CO3 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 4113 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Robotics and Automation |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Elective |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 3105 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides detailed information about various parts of robots and their control mechanisms. The covered topics include basic computational concepts used in robotics, various kinematics and inverse kinematics of robots, trajectory planning of robots and the control of robots for some specific applications. The motivation of the course is to develop basic knowledge of Robotics. After completing the course, the students will be able to understand the basic concepts and theory governing the programming of robots that perform autonomous tasks such as navigation and manipulation. 

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- |
| CO1 | Compare between the perception principles of various robot sensors that enable the robots to analyze their environment, reason and take appropriate actions toward the given goal | Cognitive/ Evaluate | 4 | K8 |   |  |
| CO2 | Solve problems in robot kinematics and mapping. | Cognitive/ Analyze | 2 | K4 |   |  |
| CO3 | Solve problems in robot controlling. | Cognitive/ Analyze | 2 | K4 |   |  |

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| Introduction to Robotics: Definition, Origin & Types of Robotics, Historical development of robot, Major components of Robotic system, Robot Anatomy & Degree of Freedom, Robotics and AI |   1 |   CO3 |
| Spatial Transformations: Cartesian Co-ordinate, Reference Frame, Transformation Matrix, Homogeneous Transformation, Inverse Transformation, Graphs, Co-ordinate system overview | 1 |   CO2 |
| Power Sources and Sensors: Hydraulic, pneumatic and electric drives, determination of HP of motor and gearing ratio, variable speed arrangements, path determination, micro machines in robotics, machine vision, ranging, laser, acoustic, magnetic, fiber optic and tactile sensors. | 2 | CO1 |
| Manipulators, Actuators, and Grippers: Manipulators, Classification, Construction of manipulators, manipulator dynamics and force control, electronic and pneumatic manipulator control, End effectors, Loads and Forces, Grippers, design considerations, Robot motion Control, Position Sensing | 2 | CO1 |
| Robot Kinematics Basics: Forward Kinematics, Inverse Kinematics. Velocities, Static Forces, acceleration of rigid bodies and Jacobians | 2 | CO2 |
| Robotic mapping: localization, Monte Carlo localization, multi-object localization | 2 | CO2 |
| Robotic navigation and locomotion: motion planning, dynamics, linear control, non-linear control, Force control, Compliance | 2 |   CO3 |
| Trajectory/Path Planning: Path planning for mobile robots, path planning for industrial robots. | 2 |   CO3 |
| Robot Programming Languages and Systems, Simulation and Off-Line Programming | 1 | CO2, CO3 |
| Case Studies: Multiple robots, Machine Interface, Uncertainty, Robots in Manufacturing and not-Manufacturing Application, Robot Cell Design, Mechanical Design of robots, Selection of a Robot | 1 | CO1 |

**Rationale:** The contents of the course include experimental work with locomotion, robot kinematics, mobile robot kinematics, actuating, sensing, path planning and obstacle avoidance to provide hands on experience so that the students can learn the problem-solving techniques through competition-based applications and projects of robotics principles. |  Course Code:  CSE 4115 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** UI/UX Design |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Elective |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 3203 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** The course introduces the novice to a cycle of discovery and evaluation and a set of techniques that meet the user's needs. The course mantra is that “Design is a systematic and data driven process.” Students will gain an understanding of the critical importance of user interface and user experience design. They will also learn industry-standard methods for how to approach the design of a user interface and user experience along with key theories and frameworks that underlie the design of most interfaces you use today. This course will demonstrate how the costs of bad design can often be severe (in user experience, money, and even human lives). 

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- |
| CO1 | Creating new designs from scratch. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO5 | WK6 |   |  |
| CO2 | Analyzing existing design and suggest improvements | Cognitive/ Understand | PO2 | WK3 |   |  |

|  Course Code:  CSE 4116 |  |  Credit Hour:  2 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |

|  **Course Status:** Elective |  |  **Course Type:**   Sessional |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** The course introduces the novice to a cycle of discovery and evaluation and a set of techniques that meet the user's needs. The course mantra is that “Design is a systematic and data driven process.” Students will gain an understanding of the critical importance of user interface and user experience design and practically demonstrate them. They will also work on industry-standard methods for how to approach the design of a user interface and user experience along with key theories and frameworks that underlie the design of most interfaces you use today.

| CO1 | Creating new UI design from scratch. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO5 | WK6 | WP1 | EA1 |
| CO2 | Gain practical experience in UX Design | Cognitive/ Understand | PO2 | WK3 | WP2 | EA3 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 4201 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Cryptography and Network Security |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2205 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course covers cryptography overview and terminologies. Symmetric Ciphers: Symmetric Cipher Model, Substitution Techniques, Transposition Techniques, Steganography, Simplified DES, Block Cipher Principles, the Data Encryption Standard, the Strength of DES, Block Cipher Design Principles, Evaluation Criteria for AES, the AES Cipher, Triple DES, Key Distribution. Public- Key Encryption: Principles of Public-Key Cryptosystems, the RSA Algorithm, Key Management. Message Authentication: Authentication Requirements, Authentication Functions, Message Authentication Codes, Hash Functions, MD5 Message Digest Algorithm, Secure Hash Algorithm, Digital Signatures, Authentication Protocols. Pretty Good Privacy, S/MIME, IP Security Architecture, Authentication Header, Encapsulating Security Payload, Web Security Considerations, Secure Socket Layer and Transport Layer Security. After completion of this course students will be able to secure a message over insecure channel by various means and to learn about how to maintain the Confidentiality, Integrity and Availability of data. 

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Describe the fundamentals of symmetric and asymmetric cryptography | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO2 | Discuss public key-based encryption technique with proper key management | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply message authentication process for network security | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

| ----- | :---: | :---: |

| Block ciphers (Block Cipher Models), Fiestel Cipher, Simplified data encryption standards, DES, Double DES, Triple DES. | 2 | CO1 |
| Public-Key Encryption: Principles of Public-Key Cryptosystems, The RSA Algorithm, Diffie Hellman Key exchange algorithm. Key Distribution, Key Management | 3 | CO2 |
| Message Authentication: Authentication Requirements, Authentication Functions, Message Authentication Codes, Web Security Considerations, Secure Socket Layer and Transport Layer Security. | 3 | CO3 |
| Hash Functions, MD5 Message Digest Algorithm, Secure Hash Algorithm, Digital Signatures, | 2 | CO3 |
| Authentication Protocols. Pretty Good Privacy, S/MIME, IP Security Architecture, Authentication Header | 2 | CO3 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 4203 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Artificial Intelligence |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2203, STA 2207  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** To realize the intelligent human behaviors on a computer. The ultimate goal of AI is to make a computer that can learn, plan, and solve problems autonomously. In this course, we will study the most fundamental knowledge for understanding AI. We will introduce Fundamental of Artificial Intelligence, some basic search algorithms for problem solving, knowledge representation and reasoning, Uncertainty, Fuzzy logic, Expert System, Robotics, Natural language processing and neural networks. 

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Discuss about the Artificial Intelligence (AI) problem space and related searching algorithms. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO2 | Use the concept of knowledge representation with its application in AI implementation. | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO3 | Analyze the learning approaches to achieve the AI system. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO4 | WK8 |  |  |
| CO4 | Design artificial neural network (ANN) suitable for pattern classification. | Cognitive/ Create | PO9 |   |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO4 |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Fundamental of Artificial Intelligence:** Definition of Al, Historical Development of Al, Application of Al, Intelligent agents – Structure of agents and its functions, Problem spaces and Search Algorithms- Breadth First Search, Depth First Search, Iterative Deepening Search, Hill Climbing, Heuristic Search, A\* Algorithm, Greedy Best First Search, the Minimax Algorithm, Constraint Satisfaction Problems. | 4 | CO1 |
| **Knowledge Representation:** Approaches and issues in knowledge representation- Knowledge \- Based Agent- Propositional Logic – Predicate logic – Unification – Resolution \- Weak slot \- filler structure – Strong slot \- filler structure. | 3 | CO2 |
| **Reasoning under Uncertainty**: Bayesian Reasoning, Naive Bayes Probability Theory, Dempster-Shafer Theory, Algorithm, Fuzzy Logic, Fuzzy Set Theory. | 3 | CO3 |
| **Introduction to selected topics in AI:** Expert Systems. Robotics, Natural Language Processing., Neural Networks, Forms of learning \- inductive learning \- Reinforcement Learning \- learning decision trees \- Neural Net learning and Genetic learning. Features of Al Programming Languages, Major Al Programming Languages: Python and Prolog, Implementation of AI Algorithms Through Python and Prolog. | 4 | CO4 |

## **Table \- II**

|  Course Code:  CSE 4207 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Elective |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 4101 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** The purpose of this course is to provide the students a string theoretical grounding of computer vision and pattern recognition. Students will get to apply their previously learned image processing knowledge to computer vision applications. Students will learn different image feature extraction technique and learn their tradeoffs and will also understand different image representation techniques. The purpose of this course is also to introduce the students with computer vision techniques. The core component of the computer vision part will be convolutional neural network. The course will make students capable of choosing right architecture, hyper parameter and optimization algorithms. 

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Apply image feature extraction techniques | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK4 |   |   |
| CO2 | Design convolutional neural networks | Cognitive/ Design | PO3 | WK5 | WP1 | EA1 |
| CO3 | Analyze a complete computer vision system | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO4 | WK8 |   |   |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Image formation:** Geometric primitives and transformation, photometric image formation, sampling and aliasing |       	2 | CO1 |
| **Basic image processing technique review**: point operations, correlation and convolution in spatial domain, filter masks, 2D Fourier transform, filtering in frequency domain |       	2 | CO2 |
| **Feature detection**: edge detection, image segmentation, Hough transformation, active contours, split and merge, mean shift and mode finding, graph cuts and energy-based methods. |       	2 | CO2 |
| **Dense motion estimation**: translational alignment, parametric motion, spline-based motion, optical flow |          1 | CO2 |
| **Image based rendering:** view interpolation, light fields and lumigraphs, environment matters. |      	1 | CO2 |
| **Pattern recognition:** Linear classifiers, loss function optimization, nonlinear classifiers, kernel methods, |       	1 | CO3 |
| **Loss function optimization**: Image feature optimization, gradient descent and other advanced 2nd order optimization techniques. | 1 | CO3 |
| **Neural networks:** multilayer perceptron, loss function of the MLP network. Convolutional neural networks: the structure of the network, convolution and pooling. | 2 | CO3 |
| **Training and hyper parameter tuning:** automatic differentiation using backpropagation, activation functions, batch processing, image normalization, transfer learning, update rules, tuning learning parameters, data augmentation | 2 | CO3 |

| COs | Instructional Strategy | Assessment Strategy |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Lecture notes, readings from books | Class test, midterm examination |
| CO2 | Lecture notes, readings from books | Assignment, midterm examination |
| CO3 | Lecture notes, readings from books | Class test, Final examination |

|  |  Final Examination  | 40%  |  |
| **Total** |  | **100%** |  |

|  Course Code:  CSE 4208 |  |  Credit Hour:  2 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |

**Rationale:** To provide hands-on experience in the field of computer vision and pattern analysis. To implement different computer vision and pattern recognition algorithms. Implement different image processing techniques and their trade-offs for computer vision applications. To implement different image feature extraction techniques to be used in the computer vision and pattern recognition pipeline. Understand the trade-offs of different cost function optimization techniques to optimize cost functions of different classifiers. Implement a complete computer vision pipeline using a convolutional neural network and understand the weaknesses and strength of different architecture and learn hyper parameters tuning process.

| CO3 | Solve real world pattern recognition problems using convolutional neural networks | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK4 | WP3 | EA3 |

Implement basic pattern recognition techniques e.g support vector machines, neural networks, and apply them to computer vision applications Implement different cost function optimization algorithms for application in pattern recognition problems algorithms |       	5 | CO2 |

| **Intents on UIs VUIs and Mobile Apps:** Text-to-Speech Techniques Designing the Right UI Multichannel and Multi Modal UIs and Services: Android Intents and Services, Characteristics of Mobile Applications, Storing and Retrieving Data, Synchronization, and Replication of Mobile Data, Android Storing and Retrieving Data, Working with a Content Provider **Communications Via Network and the Web:** State Machine, Communications Model, Android Networking, Deciding Scope of an App, Wireless Connectivity and Mobile Apps, Android Telephony, Performance and Memory Management, Android Notifications and Alarms | 3 | CO1, CO2 |

Students will create a new, unique prototype. This will be based on their work throughout the semester and should both showcase all of the skills that they've learned throughout the semester and express a unique design vision. | 4 | CO2, CO3 |

|  **Course Status:** Elective |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 3101, STA 2207 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** Big data analytics is the use of advanced analytic techniques against very large, diverse data sets. This course will introduce the basic concepts and methodologies of big data. This course will provide a comprehensive introduction to big data ecosystem, analytical theory and method. It also familiarizes with big data analysis tools and technique and visualization. After completing the course, the students will be able to analyze large diverse data sets and drive meaningful information.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Understanding the background and knowledge of some contemporary topics in Big Data. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK4 |  |  |
| CO2 | Explain the usage of data on different big data ecosystems | Cognitive/ Understand | PO4 | WK8 |  |  |
| CO3 | Apply analytical algorithms and theories of big data analysis in problem solving | Cognitive/ Apply | PO2 | WK4 |  |  |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Introduction to Big Data:** Introduction of Big Data, Characteristics and size of Big Data, Analytics, Drivers of Big Data, Big Data Attributes, Data Structures, Big Data Ecosystem, Big Data Platforms and Data Storage. **Big Data Tools, Techniques, and Systems:** Exascale computing, HDFS, HBase, and NoSQL (Document Store, Graph DB, etc.), MapReduce, Spark, Oozie, Tez, Hive, Pig, etc.; | 3 | CO1 |
| **Introduction to Hadoop:** History of Hadoop, Apache Hadoop, Analysing Data with Unix tools, Analysing Data with Hadoop, Hadoop Streaming, Hadoop Echo System, Hadoop 1 and Hadoop 2 (YARN), IBM Big Data Strategy | 2 | CO1 |
| **Map Reduce:** Anatomy of a Map Reduce Job Run, Failures, Job Scheduling, Shuffle and Sort, Task Execution, Map Reduce Types and Formats, Map Reduce Features. | 3 |   CO2   |
| **Advanced Analytical Theory and Methods:** Hadoop/Mahout, Recommendation, Clustering, Classification, Regression | 4 | CO3 |
| **Advanced Topics:** Big Data Visualization, High-performance Networking for Big Data Movement, Big Data Scientific Workflow Management and Optimization | 2 | CO3 |

| CO4 | Evaluate DevOps methodologies, continuous integration/continuous delivery (CI/CD) frameworks, and monitoring strategies. | Cognitive / Evaluate | PO5 | WK6 |  |  |

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Introduction to Cloud Computing & Virtualization:** Cloud definitions, characteristics, deployment models (Public, Private, Hybrid, Community), service models (IaaS, PaaS, SaaS); Hypervisors, virtual machines, cloud infrastructure. | 3 | CO1 |
| **Cloud Architecture, Storage & Security:** Cloud platform components, distributed storage, object storage, block storage, networking in the cloud (VPC, load balancers, gateways), cloud security models, IAM, encryption, serverless computing principles. | 3 | CO2 |
| **Microservices Architecture & Infrastructure as Code:** Monolithic vs. Microservices design, service discovery, API gateways, Infrastructure as Code concepts, declarative configuration principles, system scaling strategies. | 4 | CO3 |
| **DevOps Principles, CI/CD & Monitoring:** DevOps culture and lifecycle, Agile integration, Continuous Integration and Continuous Deployment principles, release strategies (Blue-Green, Canary), automated testing frameworks, continuous monitoring and logging strategies. | 4 | CO4 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 4214 |  |  Credit Hour:  1.5 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Cloud Computing and DevOps Sessional |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Elective |  |  **Course Type:**   Sessional |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2205 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** Hands-on practical experience in configuring cloud instances, containerizing applications, orchestrating microservices, automating infrastructure, and constructing CI/CD deployment pipelines.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Configure and manage cloud instances, virtual networks, and storage resources using cloud management tools. | Psychomotor / Practice | PO5 | WK6 |   |   |
| CO2 | Build, deploy, and orchestrate containerized applications using Docker and Kubernetes. | Psychomotor / Precision | PO3 | WK5 | WP1 | EA1 |
| CO3 | Construct automated CI/CD pipelines and Infrastructure as Code (IaC) scripts for reliable deployments. | Psychomotor / Articulation | PO5 | WK6 | WP3 | EA3 |

| CO3 |  |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Introduction to Cloud Infrastructure:** Cloud Management Tools & Virtual Resource Provisioning: Cloud CLI setup, VM instance launching, virtual networking, block and object storage configuration. | 3 | CO1 |
| **Containerization & Microservices:** Writing Dockerfiles, Docker Compose multi-container setups, Kubernetes Pods, Services, Deployments, and ConfigMaps. | 4 | CO2 |
| **Infrastructure as Code & Automation:** Terraform manifests, Ansible playbooks, and building continuous integration/deployment pipelines with GitHub Actions or Jenkins. | 4 | CO3 |
| **Final Project & Lab Evaluation:** Comprehensive end-to-end cloud-native microservices deployment project and viva assessment. | 3 | CO1, CO2, CO3 |

|  Course Code:  CSE 4215 |  |  Credit Hour:  3 |  |  Total Marks:  100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Generative AI Systems and Autonomous Agents |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Elective |  |  **Course Type:**  Theory |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2203 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This course provides a comprehensive exploration of modern Generative Artificial Intelligence systems and Autonomous Agent architectures. It covers foundational topics including Transformer architectures, Large Language Models (LLMs), prompt engineering, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), fine-tuning techniques (PEFT, LoRA), diffusion models, multi-agent coordination frameworks, tool-use integration, and AI safety and alignment. Students will gain theoretical mastery and conceptual frameworks necessary to design, evaluate, and deploy scalable, robust autonomous intelligent agent systems.

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Explain the core architectural principles of Generative AI, Large Language Models, and Autonomous Agent systems. | Cognitive/ Understand | PO1 | WK3 |  |  |
| CO2 | Analyze prompt engineering strategies, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), and parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques for custom domain applications. | Cognitive/ Analyze | PO2 | WK4 | WP1 |  |
| CO3 | Design multi-agent workflows, memory mechanisms, and tool integration architectures for complex autonomous problem-solving. | Cognitive/ Design | PO3 | WK5 | WP3 |  |
| CO4 | Evaluate safety, alignment, ethical considerations, and performance benchmarks of autonomous agent systems. | Cognitive/ Evaluate | PO5 | WK6 | WP3 | EA1 |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO4 |  |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **Foundations of Generative AI & LLMs:** Sequence-to-sequence models, Transformer architecture (self-attention, multi-head attention, positional encoding), Encoder-only, Decoder-only, and Encoder-Decoder paradigms, tokenization, pre-training objectives, scaling laws. | 3 | CO1 |
| **Prompt Engineering & Contextual Augmentation:** In-context learning, zero-shot/few-shot prompting, Chain-of-Thought (CoT), Tree of Thoughts (ToT), Directional Stimulus Prompting; Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) fundamentals, vector databases, dense passage retrieval, semantic search, hybrid retrieval. | 3 | CO2 |
| **Fine-Tuning & Model Adaptation:** Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT), Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA, QLoRA), Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). | 2 | CO2 |
| **Autonomous Agent Architectures:** Agent definition & taxonomy, cognitive loops (Perceive-Plan-Act-Reflect), memory systems (short-term, working memory, episodic & long-term vector memory), tool-use, function calling, ReAct framework, Plan-and-Solve agents. | 3 | CO3 |
| **Multi-Agent Systems & Orchestration:** Multi-agent collaboration paradigms, task breakdown, communication protocols, consensus mechanisms, hierarchical and swarm agent architectures, orchestration frameworks. | 2 | CO3 |
| **AI Safety, Alignment & Evaluation:** Agent evaluation benchmarks, hallucinations, prompt injection & jailbreaking defense, guardrails, ethical considerations, bias, governance, future directions in Autonomous AI. | 1 | CO4 |

| COs | Instructional Strategy | Assessment Strategy |
| :---: | ----- | ----- |
| CO1 | Lecture, Case Studies, Discussion | Class Test, Midterm Exam |
| CO2 | Lecture, Technical Seminars, Problem Solving | Class Test, Assignment, Midterm Exam |
| CO3 | Lecture, System Design Exercises | Assignment, Final Exam |
| CO4 | Lecture, Ethical Case Analysis | Class Test, Presentation, Final Exam |

**Assessment Details:**

| Assessment Tools   |  | Marks (%)  |  |
| ----- | :---- | :---: | :---: |
| Continuous Assessment (CA**)** | Class Participation   | 10%   |   30%   |
|  | Class Test, Assignment, Project, Discussion, Presentation  | 20%  |  |
| Summative Assessment (SA) |  Mid-term Examination  | 30%  | 70%  |
|  |  Final Examination  | 40%  |  |
| **Total** |  | **100%** |  |

**Recommended Books:**

| 1\. | Sinan Ozdemir | : | Quick Start Guide to Large Language Models: Strategies and Best Practices for Building Model-Driven Applications, *Addison-Wesley Professional* \[Textbook\] |
| :---- | :---- | ----: | :---- |
| 2\. | Denis Rothman | : | **Transformers for Natural Language Processing and Computer Vision,** *Packt Publishing* |
| 3\. | Edward Robinson | : | **Building Autonomous AI Agents: Design Paradigms and Multi-Agent Orchestration,** *O'Reilly Media* |

|  Course Code:  CSE 4216 |  |  Credit Hour:  2 |  |  Total Marks:   100 |  |
| :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- | :---- | ----- |
|  **Course Title:** Generative AI Systems and Autonomous Agents Sessional |  |  |  |  |  |
|  **Course Status:** Core |  |  **Course Type:**   Sessional |  |  |  |
|  **Prerequisite(s):**  CSE 2102, CSE 2204 |  |  |  |  |  |

**Rationale:** This sessional course provides hands-on practical experience in building, fine-tuning, and orchestrating modern Generative AI models and Autonomous AI Agents. Through laboratory experiments and a capstone project, students learn to implement advanced prompt techniques, build end-to-end Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines using vector databases, execute parameter-efficient fine-tuning (LoRA), and develop multi-agent autonomous systems using state-of-the-art frameworks (such as LangChain, LlamaIndex, AutoGen, or CrewAI).

**Course Outcomes (COs):** At the end of the Course, students will be able to

| COs | Description | Taxonomy | POs | WK | WP | EA |
| :---: | ----- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Implement prompt engineering strategies and build RAG architectures with vector embeddings and database retrieval. | Psychomotor/ Practice | PO5 | WK6 | WP1 | EA1 |
| CO2 | Execute fine-tuning of open-weights LLMs using parameter-efficient techniques (PEFT/LoRA) on custom datasets. | Psychomotor/ Precision | PO3 | WK5 | WP2 | EA1 |
| CO3 | Construct, orchestrate, and deploy multi-agent autonomous systems capable of dynamic tool execution and goal fulfillment. | Psychomotor/ Articulation | PO5 | WK6 | WP3 | EA3 |

**Mapping of Course Outcomes (COs) and Program Outcomes (POs):**

| COs | PO1 | PO2 | PO3 | PO4 | PO5 | PO6 | PO7 | PO8 | PO9 | PO10 | PO11 | PO12 |
| :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 |  |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO2 |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
| CO3 |  |  |  |  | **✓** |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

**Course Outline:**

| Course Content | Duration (Weeks) | COs |
| ----- | :---: | :---: |
| **LLM API & Prompting Labs:** Interacting with open-source and commercial LLM APIs, programmatic prompt engineering, structured JSON outputs, function calling, implementing CoT and ToT reasoning pipelines. | 2 | CO1 |
| **Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Pipelines:** Document parsing, text chunking strategies, vector embedding generation, storing and querying in vector stores (ChromaDB / Pinecone / Qdrant), re-ranking, hybrid search implementation. | 3 | CO1 |
| **Fine-Tuning Open-Weights LLMs:** Preparing instruction-following datasets, configuring HuggingFace Transformers, executing LoRA/QLoRA fine-tuning using PEFT and TRL libraries, evaluating fine-tuned models. | 3 | CO2 |
| **Single-Agent Building with Tools:** Implementing ReAct agents, tool binding (web search, SQL execution, Python code execution), short-term and persistent conversation memory integration. | 2 | CO3 |
| **Multi-Agent Orchestration & Project:** Designing multi-agent workflows using frameworks (e.g., AutoGen, CrewAI, LangGraph), human-in-the-loop validation, automated testing & guardrails, capstone agent system deployment and presentation. | 4 | CO3 |

| COs | Instructional Strategy | Assessment Strategy |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| CO1 | Lab Manual, Hands-on Lab Exercises | Continuous Lab Performance, Quiz |
| CO2 | Hands-on Lab Exercises, Code Reviews | Lab Test, Assignment |
| CO3 | Guided Project, Collaborative Coding | Capstone Project Defense, Viva |
